Scientists identify two ages when children may be most vulnerable to screen time

📅 Nov 27, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Screen time at certain ages may be more closely tied to children's future learning and memory than previously recognized. A new study tracking children from age 1 to 8 found that those who spent more time watching screens during infancy and around the start of elementary school tended to perform worse academically at age 9 and have weaker working memory at age 10.5.

The findings suggest that when children use screens may be just as important as how much time they spend in front of them. Two ages in particular, 1 and 6, emerged as potentially sensitive periods in childhood development.

As digital devices become an increasingly common part of childhood, understanding their potential long-term effects has become a growing priority. Recommendations from the World Health Organization (WHO) and the American Academy of Pediatrics generally emphasize avoiding screen exposure during the first 18 to 24 months of life and limiting it to less than one hour a day for children ages 2 to 5. However, many young children spend considerably more time using screens.

Tracking Screen Time Throughout Childhood

Despite growing concerns about children's digital habits, previous research has produced mixed findings about the relationship between screen time and cognitive development. Many earlier studies examined children at a single point in time, concentrated on children already attending school, or did not regularly track screen habits throughout early childhood.

Following the same children over several years allows researchers to investigate whether screen exposure at particular ages is more strongly associated with later outcomes. This approach can also help researchers better account for the influence of family circumstances and environmental factors.

Scientists from Inserm and the National University of Singapore addressed these gaps in a study published in the World Journal of Pediatrics. The researchers examined data from 502 children participating in the Growing Up in Singapore Towards healthy Outcomes (GUSTO) birth cohort.

Parents reported their children's screen viewing habits at six different points between infancy and middle childhood. The researchers then evaluated the children's academic achievement and working memory several years later.

Working memory is the brain's ability to temporarily hold and manipulate information. It plays an essential role in everyday learning, allowing children to follow instructions, solve math problems, understand sentences, and keep track of information while completing tasks.

The researchers found that greater screen exposure at certain stages of childhood was associated with lower academic achievement and poorer working memory later in life. These relationships were particularly consistent among children who spent more time watching screens at age 1 and around age 6, when formal schooling typically begins.

Children who accumulated more screen time throughout childhood also tended to achieve lower academic scores. However, the strength of the associations varied depending on when the exposure occurred, suggesting that some developmental stages may be more sensitive than others.

One possible explanation is that screen viewing can take time away from activities that support cognitive development, including conversations with caregivers, imaginative play, and hands-on exploration of the physical world. These experiences provide opportunities for children to develop language, attention, memory, and problem-solving skills.

"The effect sizes we saw at age 1 were the largest among all time points we examined," the authors said. "That suggests early infancy may be a window of heightened sensitivity, when the developing brain is particularly vulnerable to the displacement of learning interactions by screen time. We were also surprised to see that while screen use at age 2 and 3 did not show significant links, the associations re-emerged at age 6 -- when children enter formal schooling. So it is not just about early years; screen use later in childhood still matters."

Although the study identifies associations rather than proving that screen time directly causes poorer cognitive or academic outcomes, the results highlight the potential importance of managing children's screen habits at different ages.

Why Even Small Differences in Screen Time Matter

According to the researchers, the results reinforce the principle that "less is better" when it comes to children's screen time.

An additional hour of daily screen viewing may not produce a noticeable difference in an individual child's academic abilities. However, when similar patterns occur across a large population, even relatively small differences in average performance could increase the number of children who experience academic difficulties.

The researchers suggest that efforts to encourage healthier screen habits could begin during infancy, with renewed emphasis around the transition to elementary school. This approach recognizes that children's developmental needs continue to change well beyond their toddler years.

The authors also stress that the amount of time children spend in front of screens is only one part of a much larger picture. Future studies should investigate whether the quality of digital content, the types of devices children use, and whether parents watch and interact with their children during screen time influence developmental outcomes.

Understanding these differences could eventually help researchers develop more targeted recommendations that consider not only how much screen time children get, but also when and how they engage with digital technology.

The GUSTO study was supported by the Singapore National Research Foundation and the Singapore Ministry of Health's National Medical Research Council (NMRC/TCR/004-NUS/2008 and NMRC/TCR/012-NUHS/2014). Additional funding came from the Agence Nationale de la Recherche (ANR) in France (iSCAN project, ANR-20-CE36-0001).

Materials provided by Zhejiang University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

Published Nov 26, 2026 11:20 PM EST

The holiday is wrapping up and that to-do list is looming. Snow is coming, too. Luckily, cordless power tools can make all those jobs easier. Battery-powered outdoor tools from EGO, Greenworks, Ryobi, Milwaukee, DeWalt, and Kobalt are marked down for Black Friday at Amazon, The Home Depot, Lowe’s, and Ace Hardware, and the best price on a mower or blower depends on which store you check. Amazon has dozens of Greenworks and Worx tools on sale along with a run of robot mowers. The Home Depot is cutting prices on Ryobi, Milwaukee, DeWalt, and Echo. Lowe’s has Kobalt 80V mowers and Craftsman V20 tools, and Ace Hardware is discounting EGO blowers, mowers, and snow blowers. Cordless snow blowers and snow shovels are part of the sale, and they have their own section below.

Getting a 40V blower with a battery in the box for under $100 is a cheap entry point into cordless yard tools. The Home Depot has the RY40LB01K at $99 ($60 off), complete with a 4Ah battery, a charger, and rated output of 550 CFM and 120 MPH. The catch is platform lock-in: Ryobi’s 40V packs do not fit its ONE+ 18V tools, so this kit makes the most sense if you plan to add other 40V gear. One practical pairing is the tool-only Ryobi 40V 12-inch snow shovel (RY40SN01B), which is $169 in the same sale.

A 16-inch bar handles storm cleanup and firewood without the weight or upkeep of a gas saw. At $159.99 ($140 off), this brushless 40V kit includes an automatic oiler, a chain brake, a 4Ah battery, and a charger, and that pack doubles as a spare if you already own Greenworks 40V tools. For heavier cutting, the Greenworks 80V 16-inch chainsaw kit is $208.99; Greenworks rates it as a 42cc gas equivalent, though it ships with a smaller 2.5Ah battery.

Lowe’s has taken $260 off this Kobalt 80V push mower, putting a 21-inch battery mower with a 5Ah pack at $349. Lowe’s rates it for yards from a half-acre to an acre, which is a bigger job than the budget mowers further down this page can handle. You do the pushing, though. The self-propelled Kobalt 80V 21-inch mower (KMP 5080D-06) with the same 5Ah battery costs $529. That 80V battery also runs the Kobalt 80V 700 CFM blower (KHB 2581-06), which is $139 as a kit.

A powered snow shovel fills the gap between a plastic scoop and a full-size snow blower. Ace Hardware has EGO’s 56V SNS1201 kit at $299 ($60 off), bundled with a 2.5Ah battery and charger. That smaller pack keeps the weight down, and the 12-inch path suits decks, steps, and walkways better than a two-car driveway. For heavier snowfall, the EGO Power+ 21-inch single-stage snow blower kit (SNT2124) is $799 at Ace Hardware. The shovel uses the same 56V batteries as EGO’s mowers and blowers, so the pack stays useful once winter ends.

Battery snow shovels start under $180 with a pack included, and full-width cordless blowers from EGO, Worx, and Litheli are marked down as well.

Cordless mower prices run from $159.99 for a 13-inch Greenworks kit to $4,499 for an EGO zero-turn rider.

Handheld battery blowers are some of the cheapest tools in this sale, with several kits costing under $140. When comparing specs, CFM measures air volume for moving big leaf piles, while MPH measures airspeed for stubborn, wet debris.

Combo kits that pair a string trimmer with a blower on one battery are some of the cheapest ways to start a cordless platform.

Mini pruning saws start at $64.98 with a battery, and 16-inch and 18-inch chainsaw kits from EGO, Greenworks, and Echo are discounted too.

Hedge trimmer kits with a battery start at about $50, and the 80V Greenworks models are marked down at Lowe’s.

Wire-free robot mowers are discounted by $250 to more than $1,200, and all of the models below map the yard without a buried boundary wire. Models using RTK rely on a satellite reference antenna that can lose signal under dense tree cover, while LiDAR models map with lasers and skip the antenna. Keep in mind that 4G cellular remote control and GPS theft tracking on some mowers require an annual subscription after an initial trial period.

"La mia caratteristica migliore penso sia il tiro, sono un attaccante mobile, riesco a legare il gioco e giocare spalle alla porta. Ha fatto un percorso incredibile, lo conosco e se lo è meritato perché ha lavorato tanto e ci ha sempre creduto".

AI made self-hosting fun again

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Jan-Lukas ElseTech, life and everything else

I’ve been self-hosting things for many years. There were times when I was quite enthusiastic about it, constantly trying out new software and tinkering with my infrastructure. But at some point, that enthusiasm faded a bit.I never really stopped self-hosting, but I became less inclined to experiment with new things. Maintaining what I already had running was enough for me.Over the last few months, however, I’ve found myself getting increasingly excited about self-hosting again. And, somewhat ironically, AI is a big reason for that.Less frictionMost of my infrastructure configuration lives in Git repositories, mainly in the form of Docker Compose files, scripts, and other configuration files. This makes it possible to use AI coding agents to work on my infrastructure in much the same way as they would work on any other software project.Whenever I want to try a new service, adjust an existing configuration, or automate something, I can now do so with considerably less effort.In the past, I would sometimes put off certain changes simply because I didn’t feel like spending an evening reading changelogs and documentation or troubleshooting issues. It’s not that I couldn’t have done these things before. But often, the amount of effort required just wasn’t worth it to me.Now, even relatively spontaneous ideas often end up becoming reality.Trying things I always found interestingThere are also technologies that I’ve always found interesting but never felt knowledgeable enough about to really make use of them.NixOS is a good example. I’ve always liked the idea of declaratively configuring an entire system; I even used it on my laptop during my Linux honeymoon phase. But getting everything configured the way I wanted was quite a hurdle. With the help of AI, I’m now making much more extensive use of NixOS on my home server.For my VPS infrastructure, I’ve also started building my own Debian-based bootc image (after thinking about it for some time).Having my entire operating system configuration defined in a Git repository and automatically building a bootable container image from it is something I find really cool. But it’s also something I probably wouldn’t have invested enough time in to get working on my own before. Even with the help of AI, it still took me half a day when I set it up a few months ago. Without AI, it would probably have taken me at least a week.AI makes experimenting with unfamiliar technologies much more approachable. I don’t necessarily need to understand every little detail before I can get started. I can learn about things along the way, ask questions, and gradually improve my configurations.Automating almost everythingSomething I’ve also finally managed to do is automate most of the build, update, and deployment processes for my infrastructure.A lot of the work involved in self-hosting isn’t particularly exciting. Keeping track of new releases, updating container image tags, building images, and deploying configuration changes are all things that need to be done regularly.I still remember what updating a service used to look like: bumping the version and adjusting the Docker Compose file on my laptop, pushing the changes to the Git remote, SSHing into the server, pulling the changes, and finally deploying them. Now, a single Git push is enough. If a push is even necessary.With Renovate, I no longer need to actively keep track of most software updates. It creates pull requests for me, and often, all I need to do is review and merge them. I can even do that on my phone while sitting on the sofa.Pushing changes or merging a pull request triggers the corresponding GitHub Actions or Forgejo Actions workflows, which take care of building and deploying everything.Of course, there’s nothing particularly groundbreaking about any of this. DevOps has been around for a long time, and none of these automation tools require AI. But AI has helped me put all these pieces together in a way that I previously never quite got around to doing.And the nice thing about automating these tasks is that the benefits extend well beyond the initial setup. I now have less tedious work to do every time I want to change something or update a service.Back to more powerful hardwareAs I’ve started hosting more things again, I’ve also moved more of my services from my VPS to my home server.For a while, I was using an HP t640 thin client as my home server. However, with more things I wanted to run, I decided to switch back to my ASRock DeskMini A300, which had been sitting unused for some time. I’m thinking about repurposing the t640 as a “router on a stick”, though.I even took the opportunity to upgrade its CPU from an AMD Ryzen 5 2400G to a Ryzen 5 PRO 4650G and replaced the stock AMD cooler with a quieter Noctua cooler. Noise was the main reason I switched to the t640 in the first place.It’s nice to put some hardware I already owned back to use rather than buying an entirely new machine. I also managed to get both the CPU and the cooler for a good price through a classifieds site.Having some additional computing power available gives me more room to experiment. And there’s something quite satisfying about having a small box at home running a growing collection of services that I use in my everyday life.Building my own toolsBut it’s not just about self-hosting existing software. AI has also changed how I approach problems for which there isn’t an existing piece of software that does exactly what I want.Previously, I would often spend quite some time searching for a suitable open-source project, comparing different alternatives, and figuring out how to make one of them work for my particular use case.These days, for smaller problems, building my own solution has become a much more attractive option.I’ve recently built tools to automatically download invoices, e-papers or Linux ISOs.I can build (or have AI build) these tools in Go, my preferred programming language, and tailor them to my exact needs.They don’t need to support every possible use case, have an elaborate user interface, or be particularly interesting to anyone other than me. I don’t need to turn every little tool into a proper open-source project with extensive documentation and support for other people’s use cases. They just need to solve my problem.What I particularly enjoy is how well this complements my increasingly automated infrastructure. Building a small tool has become easier, and so has building a container image for it, deploying it, and keeping it running.The entire process, from having an idea to having something actually running on my home server, requires much less effort than it used to.Just because I can doesn’t mean I shouldOf course, there are downsides to all of this.Every additional service is another thing that needs to be maintained. AI-generated software isn’t exempt from bugs, security issues, or technical debt. And having an AI agent make changes to my infrastructure doesn’t absolve me of the responsibility of understanding those changes.There’s also the temptation to build or deploy things simply because I can. When implementing an idea becomes so easy, it’s perhaps more important than ever to occasionally ask myself whether it’s actually worth implementing in the first place.On the other hand, self-hosting is a hobby for me. Not everything I do needs to be economically sensible or particularly useful. Sometimes, experimenting with something new is the whole point.And with much of the routine maintenance automated, adding another service doesn’t necessarily mean adding as much recurring work as it used to.A somewhat ironic development?I find it somewhat ironic that AI, a technology largely dominated by big tech companies, is helping me become a little less dependent on those very companies in other areas of my digital life.I’m aware that this also creates a new kind of dependency. But the software and infrastructure configurations I create with the help of AI can continue to exist independently of the AI services I used to create them. And since I mostly use open-weight models, I have a wide range of providers to choose from.What I appreciate most, however, isn’t necessarily the time I’m saving. It’s that I’m more inclined to actually pursue the little ideas I have.I’m trying out technologies I’ve been curious about for years. I’m automating things that I’ve wanted to automate for a long time. I’m building little tools for myself. I’m tinkering with hardware again.AI hasn’t fundamentally changed why I like self-hosting. I still enjoy having control over my digital life, learning new things, and tinkering with technology.It’s just removed a lot of the friction that made me lose some of my enthusiasm for it over the years.And now, I’m having a lot of fun with it again.

I never really stopped self-hosting, but I became less inclined to experiment with new things. I still enjoy having control over my digital life, learning new things, and tinkering with technology.It’s just removed a lot of the friction that made me lose some of my enthusiasm for it over the years.And now, I’m having a lot of fun with it again.

Most of my infrastructure configuration lives in Git repositories, mainly in the form of Docker Compose files, scripts, and other configuration files. This makes it possible to use AI coding agents to work on my infrastructure in much the same way as they would work on any other software project.Whenever I want to try a new service, adjust an existing configuration, or automate something, I can now do so with considerably less effort.In the past, I would sometimes put off certain changes simply because I didn’t feel like spending an evening reading changelogs and documentation or troubleshooting issues. It’s not that I couldn’t have done these things before. But often, the amount of effort required just wasn’t worth it to me.Now, even relatively spontaneous ideas often end up becoming reality.Trying things I always found interestingThere are also technologies that I’ve always found interesting but never felt knowledgeable enough about to really make use of them.NixOS is a good example. I’ve always liked the idea of declaratively configuring an entire system; I even used it on my laptop during my Linux honeymoon phase. But getting everything configured the way I wanted was quite a hurdle. With the help of AI, I’m now making much more extensive use of NixOS on my home server.For my VPS infrastructure, I’ve also started building my own Debian-based bootc image (after thinking about it for some time).Having my entire operating system configuration defined in a Git repository and automatically building a bootable container image from it is something I find really cool. But it’s also something I probably wouldn’t have invested enough time in to get working on my own before. Even with the help of AI, it still took me half a day when I set it up a few months ago. Without AI, it would probably have taken me at least a week.AI makes experimenting with unfamiliar technologies much more approachable. I don’t necessarily need to understand every little detail before I can get started. I can learn about things along the way, ask questions, and gradually improve my configurations.Automating almost everythingSomething I’ve also finally managed to do is automate most of the build, update, and deployment processes for my infrastructure.A lot of the work involved in self-hosting isn’t particularly exciting. Keeping track of new releases, updating container image tags, building images, and deploying configuration changes are all things that need to be done regularly.I still remember what updating a service used to look like: bumping the version and adjusting the Docker Compose file on my laptop, pushing the changes to the Git remote, SSHing into the server, pulling the changes, and finally deploying them. Now, a single Git push is enough. If a push is even necessary.With Renovate, I no longer need to actively keep track of most software updates. It creates pull requests for me, and often, all I need to do is review and merge them. I can even do that on my phone while sitting on the sofa.Pushing changes or merging a pull request triggers the corresponding GitHub Actions or Forgejo Actions workflows, which take care of building and deploying everything.Of course, there’s nothing particularly groundbreaking about any of this. DevOps has been around for a long time, and none of these automation tools require AI. But AI has helped me put all these pieces together in a way that I previously never quite got around to doing.And the nice thing about automating these tasks is that the benefits extend well beyond the initial setup. I now have less tedious work to do every time I want to change something or update a service.Back to more powerful hardwareAs I’ve started hosting more things again, I’ve also moved more of my services from my VPS to my home server.For a while, I was using an HP t640 thin client as my home server. However, with more things I wanted to run, I decided to switch back to my ASRock DeskMini A300, which had been sitting unused for some time. I’m thinking about repurposing the t640 as a “router on a stick”, though.I even took the opportunity to upgrade its CPU from an AMD Ryzen 5 2400G to a Ryzen 5 PRO 4650G and replaced the stock AMD cooler with a quieter Noctua cooler. Noise was the main reason I switched to the t640 in the first place.It’s nice to put some hardware I already owned back to use rather than buying an entirely new machine. I also managed to get both the CPU and the cooler for a good price through a classifieds site.Having some additional computing power available gives me more room to experiment. And there’s something quite satisfying about having a small box at home running a growing collection of services that I use in my everyday life.Building my own toolsBut it’s not just about self-hosting existing software. AI has also changed how I approach problems for which there isn’t an existing piece of software that does exactly what I want.Previously, I would often spend quite some time searching for a suitable open-source project, comparing different alternatives, and figuring out how to make one of them work for my particular use case.These days, for smaller problems, building my own solution has become a much more attractive option.I’ve recently built tools to automatically download invoices, e-papers or Linux ISOs.I can build (or have AI build) these tools in Go, my preferred programming language, and tailor them to my exact needs.They don’t need to support every possible use case, have an elaborate user interface, or be particularly interesting to anyone other than me. I don’t need to turn every little tool into a proper open-source project with extensive documentation and support for other people’s use cases. They just need to solve my problem.What I particularly enjoy is how well this complements my increasingly automated infrastructure. Building a small tool has become easier, and so has building a container image for it, deploying it, and keeping it running.The entire process, from having an idea to having something actually running on my home server, requires much less effort than it used to.Just because I can doesn’t mean I shouldOf course, there are downsides to all of this.Every additional service is another thing that needs to be maintained. AI-generated software isn’t exempt from bugs, security issues, or technical debt. And having an AI agent make changes to my infrastructure doesn’t absolve me of the responsibility of understanding those changes.There’s also the temptation to build or deploy things simply because I can. When implementing an idea becomes so easy, it’s perhaps more important than ever to occasionally ask myself whether it’s actually worth implementing in the first place.On the other hand, self-hosting is a hobby for me. Not everything I do needs to be economically sensible or particularly useful. Sometimes, experimenting with something new is the whole point.And with much of the routine maintenance automated, adding another service doesn’t necessarily mean adding as much recurring work as it used to.A somewhat ironic development?I find it somewhat ironic that AI, a technology largely dominated by big tech companies, is helping me become a little less dependent on those very companies in other areas of my digital life.I’m aware that this also creates a new kind of dependency. But the software and infrastructure configurations I create with the help of AI can continue to exist independently of the AI services I used to create them. And since I mostly use open-weight models, I have a wide range of providers to choose from.What I appreciate most, however, isn’t necessarily the time I’m saving. It’s that I’m more inclined to actually pursue the little ideas I have.I’m trying out technologies I’ve been curious about for years. I’m automating things that I’ve wanted to automate for a long time. I’m building little tools for myself. I’m tinkering with hardware again.AI hasn’t fundamentally changed why I like self-hosting. And there’s something quite satisfying about having a small box at home running a growing collection of services that I use in my everyday life.Building my own toolsBut it’s not just about self-hosting existing software. AI has also changed how I approach problems for which there isn’t an existing piece of software that does exactly what I want.Previously, I would often spend quite some time searching for a suitable open-source project, comparing different alternatives, and figuring out how to make one of them work for my particular use case.These days, for smaller problems, building my own solution has become a much more attractive option.I’ve recently built tools to automatically download invoices, e-papers or Linux ISOs.I can build (or have AI build) these tools in Go, my preferred programming language, and tailor them to my exact needs.They don’t need to support every possible use case, have an elaborate user interface, or be particularly interesting to anyone other than me. I don’t need to turn every little tool into a proper open-source project with extensive documentation and support for other people’s use cases. They just need to solve my problem.What I particularly enjoy is how well this complements my increasingly automated infrastructure. Building a small tool has become easier, and so has building a container image for it, deploying it, and keeping it running.The entire process, from having an idea to having something actually running on my home server, requires much less effort than it used to.Just because I can doesn’t mean I shouldOf course, there are downsides to all of this.Every additional service is another thing that needs to be maintained. AI-generated software isn’t exempt from bugs, security issues, or technical debt. And having an AI agent make changes to my infrastructure doesn’t absolve me of the responsibility of understanding those changes.There’s also the temptation to build or deploy things simply because I can. When implementing an idea becomes so easy, it’s perhaps more important than ever to occasionally ask myself whether it’s actually worth implementing in the first place.On the other hand, self-hosting is a hobby for me. Not everything I do needs to be economically sensible or particularly useful. Sometimes, experimenting with something new is the whole point.And with much of the routine maintenance automated, adding another service doesn’t necessarily mean adding as much recurring work as it used to.A somewhat ironic development?I find it somewhat ironic that AI, a technology largely dominated by big tech companies, is helping me become a little less dependent on those very companies in other areas of my digital life.I’m aware that this also creates a new kind of dependency. But the software and infrastructure configurations I create with the help of AI can continue to exist independently of the AI services I used to create them. And since I mostly use open-weight models, I have a wide range of providers to choose from.What I appreciate most, however, isn’t necessarily the time I’m saving. It’s that I’m more inclined to actually pursue the little ideas I have.I’m trying out technologies I’ve been curious about for years. I’m automating things that I’ve wanted to automate for a long time. I’m building little tools for myself. I’m tinkering with hardware again.AI hasn’t fundamentally changed why I like self-hosting. I still enjoy having control over my digital life, learning new things, and tinkering with technology.It’s just removed a lot of the friction that made me lose some of my enthusiasm for it over the years.And now, I’m having a lot of fun with it again.

NixOS is a good example. I’ve always liked the idea of declaratively configuring an entire system; I even used it on my laptop during my Linux honeymoon phase. But getting everything configured the way I wanted was quite a hurdle. With the help of AI, I’m now making much more extensive use of NixOS on my home server.For my VPS infrastructure, I’ve also started building my own Debian-based bootc image (after thinking about it for some time).Having my entire operating system configuration defined in a Git repository and automatically building a bootable container image from it is something I find really cool. But it’s also something I probably wouldn’t have invested enough time in to get working on my own before. Even with the help of AI, it still took me half a day when I set it up a few months ago. Without AI, it would probably have taken me at least a week.AI makes experimenting with unfamiliar technologies much more approachable. I don’t necessarily need to understand every little detail before I can get started. I can learn about things along the way, ask questions, and gradually improve my configurations.Automating almost everythingSomething I’ve also finally managed to do is automate most of the build, update, and deployment processes for my infrastructure.A lot of the work involved in self-hosting isn’t particularly exciting. Keeping track of new releases, updating container image tags, building images, and deploying configuration changes are all things that need to be done regularly.I still remember what updating a service used to look like: bumping the version and adjusting the Docker Compose file on my laptop, pushing the changes to the Git remote, SSHing into the server, pulling the changes, and finally deploying them. Now, a single Git push is enough. If a push is even necessary.With Renovate, I no longer need to actively keep track of most software updates. It creates pull requests for me, and often, all I need to do is review and merge them. I can even do that on my phone while sitting on the sofa.Pushing changes or merging a pull request triggers the corresponding GitHub Actions or Forgejo Actions workflows, which take care of building and deploying everything.Of course, there’s nothing particularly groundbreaking about any of this. DevOps has been around for a long time, and none of these automation tools require AI. But AI has helped me put all these pieces together in a way that I previously never quite got around to doing.And the nice thing about automating these tasks is that the benefits extend well beyond the initial setup. I now have less tedious work to do every time I want to change something or update a service.Back to more powerful hardwareAs I’ve started hosting more things again, I’ve also moved more of my services from my VPS to my home server.For a while, I was using an HP t640 thin client as my home server. However, with more things I wanted to run, I decided to switch back to my ASRock DeskMini A300, which had been sitting unused for some time. I’m thinking about repurposing the t640 as a “router on a stick”, though.I even took the opportunity to upgrade its CPU from an AMD Ryzen 5 2400G to a Ryzen 5 PRO 4650G and replaced the stock AMD cooler with a quieter Noctua cooler. Noise was the main reason I switched to the t640 in the first place.It’s nice to put some hardware I already owned back to use rather than buying an entirely new machine. I also managed to get both the CPU and the cooler for a good price through a classifieds site.Having some additional computing power available gives me more room to experiment. And there’s something quite satisfying about having a small box at home running a growing collection of services that I use in my everyday life.Building my own toolsBut it’s not just about self-hosting existing software. I still enjoy having control over my digital life, learning new things, and tinkering with technology.It’s just removed a lot of the friction that made me lose some of my enthusiasm for it over the years.And now, I’m having a lot of fun with it again.

A lot of the work involved in self-hosting isn’t particularly exciting. Keeping track of new releases, updating container image tags, building images, and deploying configuration changes are all things that need to be done regularly.I still remember what updating a service used to look like: bumping the version and adjusting the Docker Compose file on my laptop, pushing the changes to the Git remote, SSHing into the server, pulling the changes, and finally deploying them. Now, a single Git push is enough. If a push is even necessary.With Renovate, I no longer need to actively keep track of most software updates. It creates pull requests for me, and often, all I need to do is review and merge them. I can even do that on my phone while sitting on the sofa.Pushing changes or merging a pull request triggers the corresponding GitHub Actions or Forgejo Actions workflows, which take care of building and deploying everything.Of course, there’s nothing particularly groundbreaking about any of this. DevOps has been around for a long time, and none of these automation tools require AI. But AI has helped me put all these pieces together in a way that I previously never quite got around to doing.And the nice thing about automating these tasks is that the benefits extend well beyond the initial setup. I now have less tedious work to do every time I want to change something or update a service.Back to more powerful hardwareAs I’ve started hosting more things again, I’ve also moved more of my services from my VPS to my home server.For a while, I was using an HP t640 thin client as my home server. However, with more things I wanted to run, I decided to switch back to my ASRock DeskMini A300, which had been sitting unused for some time. I’m thinking about repurposing the t640 as a “router on a stick”, though.I even took the opportunity to upgrade its CPU from an AMD Ryzen 5 2400G to a Ryzen 5 PRO 4650G and replaced the stock AMD cooler with a quieter Noctua cooler. Noise was the main reason I switched to the t640 in the first place.It’s nice to put some hardware I already owned back to use rather than buying an entirely new machine. I also managed to get both the CPU and the cooler for a good price through a classifieds site.Having some additional computing power available gives me more room to experiment. And there’s something quite satisfying about having a small box at home running a growing collection of services that I use in my everyday life.Building my own toolsBut it’s not just about self-hosting existing software. AI has also changed how I approach problems for which there isn’t an existing piece of software that does exactly what I want.Previously, I would often spend quite some time searching for a suitable open-source project, comparing different alternatives, and figuring out how to make one of them work for my particular use case.These days, for smaller problems, building my own solution has become a much more attractive option.I’ve recently built tools to automatically download invoices, e-papers or Linux ISOs.I can build (or have AI build) these tools in Go, my preferred programming language, and tailor them to my exact needs.They don’t need to support every possible use case, have an elaborate user interface, or be particularly interesting to anyone other than me. I don’t need to turn every little tool into a proper open-source project with extensive documentation and support for other people’s use cases. They just need to solve my problem.What I particularly enjoy is how well this complements my increasingly automated infrastructure. Building a small tool has become easier, and so has building a container image for it, deploying it, and keeping it running.The entire process, from having an idea to having something actually running on my home server, requires much less effort than it used to.Just because I can doesn’t mean I shouldOf course, there are downsides to all of this.Every additional service is another thing that needs to be maintained. AI-generated software isn’t exempt from bugs, security issues, or technical debt. And having an AI agent make changes to my infrastructure doesn’t absolve me of the responsibility of understanding those changes.There’s also the temptation to build or deploy things simply because I can. When implementing an idea becomes so easy, it’s perhaps more important than ever to occasionally ask myself whether it’s actually worth implementing in the first place.On the other hand, self-hosting is a hobby for me. Not everything I do needs to be economically sensible or particularly useful. Sometimes, experimenting with something new is the whole point.And with much of the routine maintenance automated, adding another service doesn’t necessarily mean adding as much recurring work as it used to.A somewhat ironic development?I find it somewhat ironic that AI, a technology largely dominated by big tech companies, is helping me become a little less dependent on those very companies in other areas of my digital life.I’m aware that this also creates a new kind of dependency. But the software and infrastructure configurations I create with the help of AI can continue to exist independently of the AI services I used to create them. And since I mostly use open-weight models, I have a wide range of providers to choose from.What I appreciate most, however, isn’t necessarily the time I’m saving. It’s that I’m more inclined to actually pursue the little ideas I have.I’m trying out technologies I’ve been curious about for years. I’m automating things that I’ve wanted to automate for a long time. I’m building little tools for myself. I’m tinkering with hardware again.AI hasn’t fundamentally changed why I like self-hosting. But the software and infrastructure configurations I create with the help of AI can continue to exist independently of the AI services I used to create them. And since I mostly use open-weight models, I have a wide range of providers to choose from.What I appreciate most, however, isn’t necessarily the time I’m saving. It’s that I’m more inclined to actually pursue the little ideas I have.I’m trying out technologies I’ve been curious about for years. I’m automating things that I’ve wanted to automate for a long time. I’m building little tools for myself. I’m tinkering with hardware again.AI hasn’t fundamentally changed why I like self-hosting. I still enjoy having control over my digital life, learning new things, and tinkering with technology.It’s just removed a lot of the friction that made me lose some of my enthusiasm for it over the years.And now, I’m having a lot of fun with it again.

For a while, I was using an HP t640 thin client as my home server. However, with more things I wanted to run, I decided to switch back to my ASRock DeskMini A300, which had been sitting unused for some time. I’m thinking about repurposing the t640 as a “router on a stick”, though.I even took the opportunity to upgrade its CPU from an AMD Ryzen 5 2400G to a Ryzen 5 PRO 4650G and replaced the stock AMD cooler with a quieter Noctua cooler. Noise was the main reason I switched to the t640 in the first place.It’s nice to put some hardware I already owned back to use rather than buying an entirely new machine. I also managed to get both the CPU and the cooler for a good price through a classifieds site.Having some additional computing power available gives me more room to experiment. And there’s something quite satisfying about having a small box at home running a growing collection of services that I use in my everyday life.Building my own toolsBut it’s not just about self-hosting existing software. AI has also changed how I approach problems for which there isn’t an existing piece of software that does exactly what I want.Previously, I would often spend quite some time searching for a suitable open-source project, comparing different alternatives, and figuring out how to make one of them work for my particular use case.These days, for smaller problems, building my own solution has become a much more attractive option.I’ve recently built tools to automatically download invoices, e-papers or Linux ISOs.I can build (or have AI build) these tools in Go, my preferred programming language, and tailor them to my exact needs.They don’t need to support every possible use case, have an elaborate user interface, or be particularly interesting to anyone other than me. I don’t need to turn every little tool into a proper open-source project with extensive documentation and support for other people’s use cases. They just need to solve my problem.What I particularly enjoy is how well this complements my increasingly automated infrastructure. Building a small tool has become easier, and so has building a container image for it, deploying it, and keeping it running.The entire process, from having an idea to having something actually running on my home server, requires much less effort than it used to.Just because I can doesn’t mean I shouldOf course, there are downsides to all of this.Every additional service is another thing that needs to be maintained. AI-generated software isn’t exempt from bugs, security issues, or technical debt. And having an AI agent make changes to my infrastructure doesn’t absolve me of the responsibility of understanding those changes.There’s also the temptation to build or deploy things simply because I can. When implementing an idea becomes so easy, it’s perhaps more important than ever to occasionally ask myself whether it’s actually worth implementing in the first place.On the other hand, self-hosting is a hobby for me. Not everything I do needs to be economically sensible or particularly useful. Sometimes, experimenting with something new is the whole point.And with much of the routine maintenance automated, adding another service doesn’t necessarily mean adding as much recurring work as it used to.A somewhat ironic development?I find it somewhat ironic that AI, a technology largely dominated by big tech companies, is helping me become a little less dependent on those very companies in other areas of my digital life.I’m aware that this also creates a new kind of dependency. But the software and infrastructure configurations I create with the help of AI can continue to exist independently of the AI services I used to create them. And since I mostly use open-weight models, I have a wide range of providers to choose from.What I appreciate most, however, isn’t necessarily the time I’m saving. But it’s not just about self-hosting existing software. AI has also changed how I approach problems for which there isn’t an existing piece of software that does exactly what I want.Previously, I would often spend quite some time searching for a suitable open-source project, comparing different alternatives, and figuring out how to make one of them work for my particular use case.These days, for smaller problems, building my own solution has become a much more attractive option.I’ve recently built tools to automatically download invoices, e-papers or Linux ISOs.I can build (or have AI build) these tools in Go, my preferred programming language, and tailor them to my exact needs.They don’t need to support every possible use case, have an elaborate user interface, or be particularly interesting to anyone other than me. I don’t need to turn every little tool into a proper open-source project with extensive documentation and support for other people’s use cases. They just need to solve my problem.What I particularly enjoy is how well this complements my increasingly automated infrastructure. Building a small tool has become easier, and so has building a container image for it, deploying it, and keeping it running.The entire process, from having an idea to having something actually running on my home server, requires much less effort than it used to.Just because I can doesn’t mean I shouldOf course, there are downsides to all of this.Every additional service is another thing that needs to be maintained. AI-generated software isn’t exempt from bugs, security issues, or technical debt. And having an AI agent make changes to my infrastructure doesn’t absolve me of the responsibility of understanding those changes.There’s also the temptation to build or deploy things simply because I can. When implementing an idea becomes so easy, it’s perhaps more important than ever to occasionally ask myself whether it’s actually worth implementing in the first place.On the other hand, self-hosting is a hobby for me. Not everything I do needs to be economically sensible or particularly useful. Sometimes, experimenting with something new is the whole point.And with much of the routine maintenance automated, adding another service doesn’t necessarily mean adding as much recurring work as it used to.A somewhat ironic development?I find it somewhat ironic that AI, a technology largely dominated by big tech companies, is helping me become a little less dependent on those very companies in other areas of my digital life.I’m aware that this also creates a new kind of dependency. But the software and infrastructure configurations I create with the help of AI can continue to exist independently of the AI services I used to create them. These days, for smaller problems, building my own solution has become a much more attractive option.I’ve recently built tools to automatically download invoices, e-papers or Linux ISOs.I can build (or have AI build) these tools in Go, my preferred programming language, and tailor them to my exact needs.They don’t need to support every possible use case, have an elaborate user interface, or be particularly interesting to anyone other than me. I don’t need to turn every little tool into a proper open-source project with extensive documentation and support for other people’s use cases. They just need to solve my problem.What I particularly enjoy is how well this complements my increasingly automated infrastructure. Building a small tool has become easier, and so has building a container image for it, deploying it, and keeping it running.The entire process, from having an idea to having something actually running on my home server, requires much less effort than it used to.Just because I can doesn’t mean I shouldOf course, there are downsides to all of this.Every additional service is another thing that needs to be maintained. AI-generated software isn’t exempt from bugs, security issues, or technical debt. And having an AI agent make changes to my infrastructure doesn’t absolve me of the responsibility of understanding those changes.There’s also the temptation to build or deploy things simply because I can. Building a small tool has become easier, and so has building a container image for it, deploying it, and keeping it running.The entire process, from having an idea to having something actually running on my home server, requires much less effort than it used to.Just because I can doesn’t mean I shouldOf course, there are downsides to all of this.Every additional service is another thing that needs to be maintained. AI-generated software isn’t exempt from bugs, security issues, or technical debt. And having an AI agent make changes to my infrastructure doesn’t absolve me of the responsibility of understanding those changes.There’s also the temptation to build or deploy things simply because I can. When implementing an idea becomes so easy, it’s perhaps more important than ever to occasionally ask myself whether it’s actually worth implementing in the first place.On the other hand, self-hosting is a hobby for me. Not everything I do needs to be economically sensible or particularly useful. Sometimes, experimenting with something new is the whole point.And with much of the routine maintenance automated, adding another service doesn’t necessarily mean adding as much recurring work as it used to.A somewhat ironic development?I find it somewhat ironic that AI, a technology largely dominated by big tech companies, is helping me become a little less dependent on those very companies in other areas of my digital life.I’m aware that this also creates a new kind of dependency. But the software and infrastructure configurations I create with the help of AI can continue to exist independently of the AI services I used to create them. And since I mostly use open-weight models, I have a wide range of providers to choose from.What I appreciate most, however, isn’t necessarily the time I’m saving. It’s that I’m more inclined to actually pursue the little ideas I have.I’m trying out technologies I’ve been curious about for years. I’m automating things that I’ve wanted to automate for a long time. I’m building little tools for myself. I’m tinkering with hardware again.AI hasn’t fundamentally changed why I like self-hosting. I still enjoy having control over my digital life, learning new things, and tinkering with technology.It’s just removed a lot of the friction that made me lose some of my enthusiasm for it over the years.And now, I’m having a lot of fun with it again.

Kanye West and Bianca Censori Plan to Renew Wedding Vows, Family Member Reveals

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Travel  |  Read Source →

Kanye West and Bianca Censori are apparently ready to say "I do" all over again ... with one of Bianca's relatives spilling the beans about a reported vow renewal next month.

Bianca's aunt Kelly dropped the wedding bombshell during a call to Australian radio show "Fifi, Fev & Nick" Wednesday ... revealing that several family members are preparing to travel overseas for the couple's upcoming celebration.

Kelly explained that Ye and Bianca are already married, but they're planning to exchange vows again in December ... though there's one awkward twist ... she says she wasn't invited.

Her call came after an alleged save-the-date surfaced on X earlier this month -- listing December 20 as the big day and Beverly Hills as the location -- though the invitation has not been verified.

The date would be especially fitting ... since Ye and Bianca reportedly tied the knot on December 20, 2022, in a private ceremony in Palo Alto, California, shortly after his divorce from Kim Kardashian was finalized.

And a second trip down the aisle would mark quite the turnaround for the couple. As TMZ previously reported ... Kanye and Bianca were headed for divorce in February 2025, with sources telling us both had reached out to divorce attorneys following Ye's antisemitic social media rants.

However, the two reunited that April and have been practically inseparable ever since ... frequently stepping out together and packing on the PDA.

Neither Kanye nor Bianca has publicly confirmed the reported ceremony ... but if Kelly's right, wedding bells could be ringing again in just a few weeks.

Investing - Theory, News &amp; General • Re: Article: Do Bond Investors Get Rewarded for Taking Dur. Risk? (P. 1) Edward F. McQuarrie, William J. Bernstein, 11/6

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

[sorry for the delayed reply. We just finished up Part 2, should appear after the holiday.]
Thank you for your input.
typical.investor, I have to concur with StillGoing: your time period of 2010 - 2025 is too short to have probative value. You need at least a complete bear and bond market cycle. That's why we liked the post-1945, 80-year span: 36 years of bear, 40 years of bull, 4 years of renewed bear.
Agreed. I'm looking at the FRED H.15 data from April 1953 onward next—that's where the monthly series begins, and it also avoids the wartime yield-curve control era prior to the 1951 Fed–Treasury Accord.
In our thinking, the great 40-year bull 1981 - 2021 created the mother of all recency biases: of course bonds give you a coupon plus capital gain--that's just what bonds do! Not.

And of course, a bull market favors duration.
Agreed. It's why the period I looked at was actually rising rates overall.
Next, I don't think you can test a ladder against a constant maturity approach by switching from the Coleman / Ibbotson yield curve (which we used) to the Treasury HR 15 curve (which Shiller uses). I'd recommend actual purchase prices. These are available from the CRSP Treasury file--I access it through my university library subscription to WRDS.

That may be too heavy a lift--not everybody has access to a University library. But to go beyond a thoughtful contribution to the forum, to something you could publish, I think that's the threshold: buy the bonds at the ask, hold, handle the semi-annual coupon payments.
I don't intend to test further against constant maturity, but will instead look at a hold-to-maturity ladder vs a fund or sector (like 7-10y) using the same Treasury H.15 curve.

As an investor, I realistically do have a choice of using a ladder or fund. Constant-maturity methodology isn't something that really happens in the normal course of investing I think.
Next, remember that the 5-year maturity was not a mountain peak--it was the point of rapid flattening as far as return by maturity is concerned. Calculating a return on a 10-year ladder, with maturity of 5.5 years, and showing a dozen basis points of extra return, relative to exactly 5 years, does not move the ball down the field.
Viewing a 10-year ladder as simply "5.5 years of maturity" misses an essential return mechanism of individual bonds: pull-to-par.

A constant-maturity series buys a bond, sells it a month later, and buys back the original maturity. In a rising-rate regime, that forces repeated capital losses; in a falling-rate regime, it harvests continuous capital gains. Buying a bond and holding it to maturity doesn't work that way—your return is anchored by your starting yield and pull-to-par, with rate changes only affecting coupon reinvestment.

You can see how different those two mechanisms are in the 1953–2026 H.15 data (762 rolling 10-year windows). Let's strip out the shared starting yield and look at the correlation.

Correlation of deviations from starting yield (Realized 10y CAGR − Starting Yield):
10y CMT vs. a single 10y bond held to maturity: -0.279
10y CMT vs. a rolling 0–10y HTM ladder: +0.633

For a single 10-year bond held to maturity, the rate-change risk literally points in the opposite direction of 10y CMT. In the 1953–1981 bear market, 10y CMT lagged a buy-and-hold 10-year Treasury in 100% of rolling 10-year windows (by a median of -102 bps/yr); in the 1981–2020 bull market, it beat it in 100% of windows (by a median of +163 bps/yr).

That's why I think the "coupon plus capital gain" recency bias you noted is more a constant-maturity artifact than how hold-to-maturity Treasuries behave. Of course, I acknowledge that real-world bond funds that don't hold to maturity will also realize capital gains in bull markets and capital losses in bear markets, although likely to a lesser degree than CMT—which is why I'm testing hold-to-maturity ladders against funds.
Last, much of our analysis was focused on stock-bond portfolios, and the question of which Treasury best supplements stocks. The mean-variance charts, per Markowitz, assume regular rebalancing back to the indicated weights.

Ladders do not rebalance. You need Tobin (2-fund separation theorem), not Markowitz, given a ladder. Space constraints prevented us from going there.
I, too, am very much concerned with stock-bond portfolios. Where we differ is that I find it straightforward to rebalance with a rolling ladder inside a 60/40 portfolio:

When stocks drop: Maturing rungs and semiannual coupons provide natural cash flow to buy equities. If a sharp drop requires more than the maturing rung provides, selling the shortest remaining rung is functionally identical to selling a T-bill.

When stocks surge: Trimming equities funds new top rungs at the long end of the ladder.

Any temporary drift in average maturity from selling the front rung or adding to the back rung is largely self-correcting as the remaining rungs age. And because a rolling nominal ladder carries real duration and price risk, it isn't a Tobin risk-free asset—it's a fixed-income sleeve that rebalances Markowitz-style just fine.

I do again thank you for your input and look forward to your next work.

Statistics: Posted by typical.investor — Thu Nov 26, 2026 4:45 pm

6 YA Books That Are Better Than They Get Credit For

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Young adult fiction has produced some of the biggest literary phenomena of the 21st century, from The Hunger Games to Twilight, and this trend shows no signs of slowing. Even now, YA hits like Heartstopper and A Good Girl's Guide to Murder are fixtures on the bestseller lists.

However, there are also plenty of YA gems that are a little under-sung. Perhaps bigger franchises stole their limelight, or they had their moment in the sun but then mostly faded from popularity, or they received rave reviews but struggled to reach a wide, mainstream audience. With that in mind, here are a few titles that probably deserve a little more praise and attention than they get.

"Sometimes the most beautiful things are the most dangerous." This is a fast-paced, atmospheric fantasy novel. Our protagonist is 16-year-old orphan Juliette, who works tirelessly to gift her beloved older sister, Clare, a weeklong stay at The Splendor. It's a glamorous, magical hotel on a hill known for fulfilling its guests' wildest fantasies. However, Clare returns from the trip completely changed: cold and detached, unfeeling. Determined to uncover what broke their bond, Juliette checks into the hotel herself.

Inside, she meets Henri, a talented young illusionist employed by the hotel to manipulate guests' minds and memories to craft their "Signature Experience". As Juliette digs beneath the hotel's dazzling surface, Henri realizes he is directly tied to the heartache she is trying to undo. Their dual perspectives (alternating between viewpoint chapters) drive the mystery, while layered world-building and a nuanced magic system do the rest of the heavy lifting.

"This place is like the whole world and the whole world is like this place. So where could I go?" The Noughts & Crosses series ranks among the most thoughtful YA dystopian fiction of the last quarter-century, yet it's typically overshadowed by the likes of The Hunger Games. Set in an alternate society where Black people, known as Crosses, occupy positions of power while white people, called noughts, face systemic discrimination, the novel follows two childhood friends whose growing romantic feelings threaten to destroy their lives.

Sephy Hadley belongs to a wealthy, influential Cross family, while Callum McGregor is a nought struggling against the forces seeking to determine his future. The premise is provocative, but the book offers way more than just that. For starters, the characterization is top-notch. Neither protagonist is written as a perfect hero; Sephy often struggles with her own biases and blind spots, while Callum wrestles with growing bitterness and anger.

"The Winter Knights stick together, Quint." Part of the phenomenal Edge Chronicles fantasy series, The Winter Knights centers on Quint, a squire training at the prestigious Knights Academy in the floating city of Sanctaphrax. As an unusually harsh winter threatens the city's survival, our hero finds himself caught up in a mystery involving missing knights, political rivalries, and the increasingly desperate search for stormphrax, a remarkable substance capable of weighing down the levitating rock on which Sanctaphrax rests.

The book's greatest strength is its wonderfully bizarre fantasy setting, populated by strange creatures, eccentric scholars, and fantastical technologies that feel unlike those found in more conventional fantasy adventures. All of this is realized through Chris Riddell's brilliant illustrations. However, there's also a surprising amount of darkness beneath the whimsical surface. For instance, the story explores institutional corruption, jealousy, betrayal, and the dangers of ambition.

Four worlds and four completely different ideas of what a good life looks like. This is where you belong, what it would ask of you, and what you would get back in return.

You belong somewhere old enough that the mountains have names and somebody can still tell you who gave them. Middle-earth is for people who think the right thing is worth doing even when it will not be finished in their lifetime.

You want the sea, a crew, and absolutely nobody telling you where you are allowed to go. The Grand Line is the world for people whose main ambition in life is finding out what is over there.

You would rather be feared and useful than liked and useless. The Continent is a world where the monsters are the straightforward part of the job and the people are where it all gets complicated.

You want the myths to be real and loose in the actual world, and you want to find out at about thirteen that you were never anywhere near as ordinary as everybody kept telling you.

"All I ever wanted was for her to love me and to do something meaningful with my life." An Abundance of Katherines is generally considered John Green's worst book, though it gets a worse rap than it deserves. Fundamentally, this is an eccentric but wise coming-of-age story. It's about Colin Singleton, a former child prodigy who can solve anagrams in seconds. He's also been dumped nineteen times (he's just 17, by the way), exclusively by girls named Katherine.

Devastated after his latest breakup with Katherine XIX, Colin sets off on a spontaneous road trip from Chicago with his hilarious, Judge Judy-loving best friend, Hassan. Their aimless drive lands them in the tiny, quirky town of Gutshot, Tennessee. While staying there, Colin obsesses over inventing a mathematical theorem to predict the exact breakup date of any relationship. However, he also slowly realizes that life, friendship, and love can't be reduced to a formula.

"The entire future of western civilization depends upon me making a smiley face with this ketchup." The Recruit is the opening entry in the incredibly entertaining CHERUB series, an action-packed spy saga aimed at younger teens. It revolves around James Choke, a troubled twelve-year-old whose life falls apart following his mother's sudden death. He finds himself recruited into CHERUB, a secret British intelligence organization that trains children and teenagers to undertake dangerous undercover missions.

Although the book is primarily concerned with delivering fast-paced entertainment (including fight scenes and a little romance), author Robert Muchamore brings an unexpected degree of realism to its outrageous premise. James is frequently selfish, impulsive, and unpleasant, making him a refreshingly imperfect protagonist with a real arc. The Recruit also avoids glamorizing espionage, instead leaning into its psychological pressures and myriad ethical complications.

"The time has come to arm-wrestle some demons." This one was acclaimed on release, but you don't seem to see it come up very often in the YA conversation these days. Speak focuses on Melinda Sordino, a high school freshman who becomes a social outcast after calling the police during a summer party. Too traumatized to explain what actually happened to her at that party, Melinda retreats into near-total silence. However, her art classes gradually provide an outlet for emotions she cannot otherwise express.

What follows is an honest depiction of harm and healing, one that refuses to sugarcoat anything or offer any contrived silver linings. Plus, even in the depths of depression and isolation, the protagonist's monologue is razor-sharp and observant, as well as frequently darkly funny. In short, Speak is a modern YA classic that should be more widely read.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Scientists find a new way to study some of the ocean’s most elusive microbes

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A new study sheds light on the largely unexplored microbial world beneath the ocean and its potential ecological significance.

Most of the microbes living on the deep seafloor have never grown in a lab dish. On the way to the surface, a sample loses the high pressure and steady cold its microbes are adapted to.

The sudden change can kill the microbes before anyone gets to study them.

A robotic system called DISCUSS keeps both conditions in place, from the seafloor all the way to the lab bench where single microbes are picked out. With it, a team grew a bacterium that barely grows at ordinary air pressure.

The work was led by Jing-Chun Feng of Guangdong University of Technology in China. Zhengguang Liu, a researcher in the University of Manchester Department of Chemical Engineering, was one of the paper’s corresponding authors.

“Scientists have long struggled to grow many microorganisms found in nature under laboratory conditions, limiting our understanding of how they live and function,” said Liu.

The deep sea is home to a vast diversity of microbes, many of which have adapted to extreme pressure and near-freezing temperatures.

Some deep-sea microbes even produce enzymes and other molecules.

The molecules that deep-sea microbes create can be useful for medicines, new materials, food and energy.

Scientists say that less than one percent of deep-sea microbes can be grown with normal lab techniques.

“Deep-sea microbes are particularly fascinating because they have evolved to survive extreme pressure, cold and unusual chemical conditions,” Liu said.

The testing of this technology was carried out in the South China Sea at a depth of 4,300 feet (1,300 meters) in a cold seep location.

Cold seeps are areas of the seafloor where methane and other compounds emerge at temperatures similar to those of the surrounding water.

At this depth, the water pressure would be 1,900 pounds per square inch (13 megapascals), which is about 130 times the atmospheric pressure at sea level, and water temperature would be 39°F (4°C).

Before carrying out any sampling procedure, sensors detected the levels of methane, carbon dioxide, oxygen, acidity, temperature, and pressure on the seafloor.

After evaluation by the software, only those locations with scores related to methanotrophic microbes were sampled.

The sealed samplers kept more than 98% of the seafloor pressure on the way up.

In the lab, the system built a virtual copy of the seafloor conditions from the sensor readings. Researchers call that kind of copy a digital twin, and the machine used it as the target to hold.

When a sample moved from one chamber to the next, one pump pushed while another pulled at a matched rate. That kept the pressure from jumping during the handoff.

To grow one kind of microbe on its own, a microbiologist spreads cells thinly across a soft gel. Each cell that survives grows into a small dot called a colony.

DISCUSS performs this task inside a sealed, high‑pressure chamber. A robot arm spreads the cells with a needle, and a camera watches through a sapphire window.

A force sensor in the arm lets it press lightly enough not to tear the gel. In tests, the camera software recognized colonies with 94.6% accuracy.

The needle hit its target within 0.002 inches of the intended spot. Between tasks, the system sterilized the needle using light, keeping the pressure seal intact.

“From an engineering perspective, what excites me most is how intelligent sensing, modeling and automation can help us explore biological systems that were previously difficult to study,” said Liu.

The team recovered 34 microbes and grew each one on its own.

In 11 of them, a gene that scientists use to tell microbes apart differed enough from known reference strains that they may belong to groups not yet described.

The team took a close look at two of the 34. Both turned out to be strains of known species.

One belongs to Pseudomonas songnenensis, and the other, named HP_SR10, belongs to Bacillus cereus.

To test how much each strain depended on pressure, the researchers grew both for 11 days at five pressures, from ordinary air pressure to a little above the seafloor’s.

The Pseudomonas strain grew about as well at ordinary air pressure as it did at seafloor pressure. HP_SR10 didn’t. It grew best at the same pressure as the seep it came from, and poorly at ordinary air pressure.

The authors wrote that this strain can’t be recovered with conventional methods, which grow microbes at surface pressure.

The system also kept a whole community of microbes alive and working for 180 days.

At its center was a partnership between methane-eating archaea, single-celled microbes distinct from bacteria, and bacteria that use sulfate from seawater.

Pumps fed the community methane and nutrients and flushed out waste. That kept sulfide, a byproduct that holds these microbes back at high levels, below 4 millimoles per liter.

Around day 110, both partners became noticeably more common.

The authors wrote that communities like this one typically collapse in closed lab containers with no fresh supply.

Carbon made near the ocean surface can sink to the deep ocean, and scientists still don’t fully understand how deep-sea microbes change it.

Growing a wider range of these microbes under conditions close to home could help scientists study that process.

Only two of the 34 microbes were studied in detail. The study doesn’t describe what the 11 possibly new microbes do, or whether any make useful molecules.

All of the samples also came from a single seep. The authors noted that the design could be adapted for deeper water and for very hot places such as hydrothermal vents.

Next, the team plans to run many plates at once under different conditions and to track dissolved chemicals more closely as they change.

The researchers also plan to automate growing microbes in liquid instead of on gel.

The full study was published in the journal Nature Sensors.

Sarri: ‘Atalanta cannot always recover with comebacks’ after another wild night

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Maurizio Sarri warns Atalanta ‘cannot always expect to resolve games with these comebacks’ after a 3-2 away to Borac in the Conference League, while Gianluca Scamacca is ‘lacking confidence.’

La Dea really are turning into the most unpredictable team in Italy, as just days after an epic comeback from 4-2 down to hold Inter 4-4 in Serie A, they went even better in the Conference League.

Borac made the most of dismal defending to go 2-0 up in the first half-hour, while Atalanta wasted numerous scoring opportunities with Jonathan Rowe and Charles De Ketelaere literally missing open goals.

It was only after Borac went down to 10 men that they broke through with Nikola Krstovic, then as Sebastijan Herrera also saw red for hacking down Nicola Zalewski, the turnaround in stoppages from Sead Kolasinac and Davide Zappacosta.

“The truth is that we had a good performance and created many scoring opportunities, but unfortunately right now we have this defensive fragility that makes every match more complicated,” Sarri told Sky Sport Italia.

“If you look at the statistics, we scored three goals on four chances late on, but before that managed to miss two goals from a metre out. We made mistakes in our finishing and continue to have a few defensive problems too, so naturally it’s going to get tricky with those two elements.

“The team showed great character, as it has been doing quite a lot recently, so we have to keep working on the problems that are making our matches so complicated.”

Injuries ruled out Marco Carnesecchi, Odilon Kossounou and Isak Hien, while others are making their way back from long lay-offs.

“We’ve got to keep working, try to dig out some time to do work in training, which between injuries and the fixture list is tough, but we absolutely must find a way of working on the defence and attack. It’s about the movements and distribution, which certainly weren’t of the right level tonight, and it has been a repeated problem in recent matches.

“We cannot always expect to resolve games with these comebacks, we’ve got to stop them getting this complicated in the first place.”

Notably, Atalanta only managed to get on the scoresheet once Krstovic came off the bench to replace Gianluca Scamacca, while Jonathan Rowe was again misfiring.

“Before the season began, I’d have said Scamacca was perfectly suited to my style of football, because he is very physical and has technical quality to work with the team,” noted Sarri.

“The difference is that one striker right now is feeling very confident and optimistic, while the other is in a difficult momenta and slowly trying to rebuild. I have always said that the two forwards start on the same level, and I pick whoever is in the best form. Right now, Krstovic is doing better, so he plays more, but it could just be a moment in this season that will pass.”

There was tension before the game, with some Atalanta fans complaining that their buses were delayed crossing the border into Bosnia, then they were banned from visiting the city centre and corralled into the stadium by police.

“I want to thank our fans, who travelled a long way to be here and for 97 minutes gave us a big hand. Fortunately, we managed to repay them this evening,” added Sarri.

This result puts Atalanta mid-table in the Conference League, after two victories, a defeat away to Ajax and draw with Kairat Almaty.
