Nvidia Is the Worst-Performing "Magnificent Seven" Stock This Year. Is It a Buy Before 2027?

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Even with Jensen Huang back as CEO, Nvidia is drastically underperforming the S&P 500 and Nasdaq this year.

By Daniel Foelber – Nov 26, 2026 at 5:30PM EST

Nvidia (NVDA -3.91%) investors have been on a rather unenjoyable roller coaster in recent weeks. And every time the ride looks like it's nearing an end, there's another unexpected turn that unearths more questions than answers.

Founder and CEO for over 33 years, Jensen Huang, admitted on Nov. 8 to being unfaithful to his wife, then resigned on Nov. 12, only to be brought back as CEO less than two weeks later on Nov. 23.

That same day, President Trump was on a phone interview with CNBC and took credit for speaking with Nvidia's board and helping convince them to bring Huang back. Trump said, "I spoke to them. Let me just put it that way. And I convinced them. And I will tell you, it was a lot easier to convince them yesterday than it was a week and a half ago." Trump was referring to Nvidia's stock price and how it was easier to convince Nvidia to bring Huang back when the stock was cratering.

Nvidia has rallied substantially since Huang came back as CEO, but Huang and Nvidia still face an uphill climb of federal, state, and internal investigations linked to Nvidia misleading investors and potential national security violations regarding advanced Nvidia artificial intelligence (AI) chips leaking into China through backdoor channels.

The stock is still under $100 per share at the time of this writing and down 20% year to date -- making it the worst-performing "Magnificent Seven" stock in 2026. The Magnificent Seven consists of seven mega-cap tech-focused companies that include Nvidia, Apple (AAPL +0.43%), Microsoft (MSFT -1.25%), Alphabet (GOOG -2.02%) (GOOGL -2.13%), Amazon (AMZN -3.12%), Meta Platforms (META +3.40%), and Tesla (TSLA -1.18%).

Here's the simplest reason to buy Nvidia, and an argument for why it's best to keep the semiconductor stock on a watch list for now.

Heading into this year, some investors were concerned that Nvidia would be unable to maintain its breakneck revenue growth rate and high gross margins. But hyperscalers continued to ramp up AI capital expenditures. And Nvidia has broadened its customer base to other enterprises, AI start-ups, AI labs, and neoclouds.

As a result, Nvidia's earnings growth began outpacing its stock price growth, compressing its valuation. But then the unthinkable happened.

Nvidia's stock price has plummeted despite exceptional results and guidance for 75% revenue growth in fiscal 2028. Nvidia reported third-quarter fiscal 2027 results on Nov. 17, with revenue more than doubling from the third quarter of fiscal 2026. In the nine months ended Oct. 25, 2026, Nvidia generated $183.3 billion in net income compared to $77.11 billion for the nine months ended Oct. 26, 2025.

Nvidia is on track to surpass Alphabet as the world's most profitable company. It now trades at just 15.4 times forward earnings -- making it the cheapest Magnificent Seven stock despite having the highest gross margins and the fastest revenue growth rate.

For context, the forward P/E of the S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.25%) is 17.3 -- meaning Nvidia is now priced like a value stock.

Buying high-quality companies on sale is an excellent way to compound wealth over time. Nvidia doesn't have to deliver blowout results to impress investors. In fact, its growth rate could fall substantially, and it would still be a steal at current levels.

The issue isn't Nvidia's valuation -- it's investors' concerns about where the company could be headed over the next three to five years.

Jensen Huang's sudden resignation exposed Nvidia's extreme vulnerability to key-person risk, governance failures, and an incapable board of directors. Going forward, Nvidia will need to change its structure to be less dependent on Huang, which could make it more rigid and less innovative but also protect long-term investors.

In addition to Nvidia-specific issues, there's also the risk that AI spending could decline considerably due to higher interest rates, which have made borrowing more expensive. With democrats taking control of the Senate and the House, there may be higher regulatory hurdles to accelerating data center construction. And there's the ongoing risk that compute demand will taper off as AI models become more efficient.

Granted, Nvidia is incredibly well positioned to endure a slowdown because of its industry-leading position and rock solid balance sheet. But it could also lead to stagnating or declining earnings in the medium term, which would challenge Nvidia's valuation.

All told, Nvidia simply has too many unknowns right now. Until investors get more clarity on the findings of the investigations, the company's succession plan for Huang, and the extent of its governance overhaul, Nvidia remains a hold.

Heading into the new year, I'd rather buy high-quality Magnificent Seven stocks with a clear runway for future earnings growth like Alphabet, even if its valuation is more expensive than Nvidia's.

Xabi Alonso hits back at Jamie Carragher claim as ‘responsibility’ claimed for Chelsea defending

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Your weekly briefing on Chelsea, featuring team news and expert analysis from Dom Smith

I would like to be emailed about offers, event and updates from Evening Standard. Read our privacy notice.

Xabi Alonso says he takes the “whole responsibility” for Chelsea’s dismal defending but disagrees with his friend Jamie Carragher that Wesley Fofana and Levi Colwill will never be a good enough centre-back pairing for the Blues.

Fofana and Colwill were heavily criticised for their performances last Saturday as the west Londoners were thrashed 4-1 at home to Leeds in the Premier League.

The defeat exposed further weaknesses in Alonso’s defence and proved the worst possible response to their disappointing previous outing, the 1-0 defeat to Sunderland before the international break.

Chelsea head to the City Ground to face Nottingham Forest on Friday night eager to get back to winning ways.

Analysing the Leeds loss, Carragher said on Sky Sports: “Chelsea have got a massive problem at centre-back and if they don’t change it in January, they’re going to have a big problem in terms of where they feel they should be this season with the Champions League.”

Speaking about Fofana and Colwill, he added: “As a partnership, this will never work because they’re not physical enough and there’s no real leader there.”

Asked whether he had seen these comments from his former Liverpool team-mate, Alonso said he had not, before adding: “I must disagree. I disagree with Carra. For sure, you can have bad games. We were not good [against Leeds]. But I think I’ve seen them [Colwill and Fofana] play well, and I’ve seen them show good levels.

“This is the goal and the challenge – to be consistent in the performance and to be consistent in the basics and not to drop in certain games when we are facing a tough opponent.”

Alonso, speaking more broadly about Chelsea’s poor defending, added: “There are no excuses not to do better. We are in the process. Taking steps, taking a step forward but until it is totally in the DNA of the team it takes time.

“I take the whole responsibility of the team and how they perform. We need to show [improvement against Forest], not just talk. Talk is cheap. It’s about performing on the pitch.

“We want to be back on that more solid feeling that we had. This was hopefully one-[off], and we don’t want to repeat it.”

Jesse Plemons on the ‘Honor’ of Playing Plutarch in ‘The Hunger Games’ After Philip Seymour Hoffman and Debating Director Francis Lawrence Over the Character’s Motivations

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

“The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping” might be Haymitch Abernathy’s prequel movie, but Oscar nominee Jesse Plemons was the first person cast in the film.

Plemons plays Plutarch Heavensbee, a Capitol insider, who becomes a key figure in the rebellion that sparks in the latter half of Suzanne Collins’ original trilogy of dystopian novels. In the second book, “Catching Fire,” and its 2013 film adaptation, Plutarch is introduced as the Head Gamemaker, tasked with creating the brutal trials in the Hunger Games. (The late Philip Seymour Hoffman originated the role.) At the end of that story, Katniss Everdeen (Jennifer Lawrence) learns that Plutarch was really scheming from inside the establishment to help support the rebels. Related Stories James Talarico Crashes ‘SNL’ Cold Open: ‘I’m the Guy Who’s Been Texting You Every Day Asking for $5’ ‘SNL’ Weekend Update Brutally Roasts Elon Musk Over 'Epic’ Midterms Defeat

But “Sunrise on the Reaping” takes place 25 years before those events, so casting the younger Plutarch was key.

“I’m a huge fan of Jesse’s,” franchise director Francis Lawrence tells Variety. “Had never worked with him before, and had always wanted to, and thought he would be perfect.”

Plemons, of course, is one of the blockbuster movie’s most accomplished stars — one of the star-studded ensemble’s seven Oscar nominees: Jennifer Lawrence and Kieran Culkin are winners, with Glenn Close receiving an honorary Oscar earlier this month.

“We had a lot of fun casting all these people fans are familiar with for this movie. But with [Plutarch] specifically, there was a lot more development,” Lawrence adds, sharing that he and “Hunger Games” producer Nina Jacobson spent considerable time working with Collins on Plutarch’s arc.

“He’s an infiltrator,” Lawrence explains. “He’s the guy at the beginning of the formation of the resistance, and at the same time, has to get to the place where by ‘Catching Fire’ he’s at Snow’s side and so trusted. So, it was on us to figure out how to portray Plutarch in a way that is believable. And that was fun because we were adding scenes that weren’t in the book.”

Book readers might’ve noticed a new Plutarch scene in the “Sunrise on the Reaping” movie — where Plemons appears bruised and bloodied after being brutally interrogated by President Snow (Ralph Fiennes), who’s learned of a secret plot to sabotage the 50th Hunger Games.

“That was a keystone scene for us, and we went over and over and over different approaches of it,” Lawrence says. “How can we have him be active on these two distinct paths: one is, ‘How can I keep this resistance alive, but a secret?’ And ‘How can I seem utterly loyal and faithful to Snow, who is very, very smart?’ It’s become one of my favorite scenes in the movie.”

Lawrence kicked off conversations with Plemons by sending Collins’ unpublished manuscript. The actor cracked open the book, but because he was also working on “Digger,” he hadn’t made it to the final pages before the finished script came in.

“I read the script, and then finished the book, and was surprised by how engaging it was and how complex the character was,” Plemons tells Variety over the phone from his home in Texas. “And I was really blown away by the script and the book.”

Indeed, Plutarch is a meaty character. But he also had to consider that the role was originated by Hoffman, one of his favorite actors of all time, who’d also played his father in 2012’s “The Master.” As such, Plemons knew saying yes would come with plenty of questions from reporters about how it felt to continue the late Hoffman’s “legacy” after the actor’s unexpected death in 2014. Even after filming the part, Plemons hasn’t quite ironed out how to articulate his feelings on such a lofty question.

“That’s a tough one to answer,” Plemons replies, thoughtfully, when I ask how he feels about all of that. “I’m definitely honored to have the opportunity to play this character. I wish I had a better answer for you, but it’s complicated.”

Less complicated are his feelings about the “Hunger Games” franchise’s larger message, which was a key factor in his decision to take the part.

“I find it a big accomplishment when a film or a book that has such a wide appeal is able to tackle real-world themes and issues everyone feels and relates to, that connect to all of our lives,” Plemons says.

Read on as Plemons explains how he approached Plutarch’s prequel arc, including what he garnered from Hoffman’s performance, and why he and Lawrence debated over the complex character’s true motivations.

Many things. One, just the nature of doing a prequel like this. It was interesting knowing where the character ends up and how all these relationships progress, and then realizing there was actually more space to play and create ourselves. To fill in this very large blank over the course of 25 years. And there was something, just in the scope of the amount of time that he has been fighting and infiltrating the capital, there’s a sort of double agent aspect.

There’s a sort of philosopher side to him. There’s most definitely an importance on knowledge that he’s trying to instill in Haymitch. And at the same time, what I was most interested in was his very pragmatic sense of hope, which is not at all shrouded in any sort of rose-colored idealism. It’s very rooted in the reality of how difficult and almost impossible it’s going to be, and I was just really intrigued by that on a personal level.

The other interesting aspect is that he winds up being with the good guys, the resistance. But even still, in his relationship with Haymitch, I feel like I was constantly questioning — and I feel like the audience probably will too — just how expendable he feels Haymitch is for the greater good. All the while, he’s at least, to me, growing pretty fond of him as the story goes on. So there were just a lot of complexities that I really enjoyed.

That was the first sequence I shot, which was great. It’s obvious he’s incredibly intelligent. He’s able to sort of play this role. And I really loved that sequence with Glenn [Close]. I’m assuming they’ve known each other practically all of Plutarch’s life; she’s probably like a crazy aunt in some way. And it was really fun to play with maniuplating Drusilla without her knowing it.

It’s interesting. Obviously, his agenda is to prove himself to Snow and everyone in the Capitol. And at the same time, there must be something kind of alluring about pulling this [rebellion] off. It was really interesting, and Francis definitely allowed for some space, especially in the sequence where we’re filming Haymitch saying goodbye. We brought in a little more of the book there and a little more of Plutarch handling the camera operator, being a little more hands-on than was in the scene.

That scene, because it was a new addition that wasn’t in the book, was something that I could definitely see the need for — to see where Plutarch was in all of this. Aside from seeing in the library earlier on, it’s the only time you get to see Plutarch with Snow, and it was just an absolute thrill getting to work with Ralph.

It’s such a game of intellect between the two. Plutarch is trying to gauge how much Snow knows, whether he knows that he’s involved and who he knows is involved. At the same time, he’s trying to double down and act as if he’s truly working in the interest of the Capitol. We played around a lot with that scene.

I don’t think there’s like a black-or-white, or cut-and-dry, easy way of categorizing Plutarch or his motivations. The main debate Francis and I were in was whether or not his motivations were rooted in some sort of self-centeredness. A kind of wanting to be the hero, or just wanting to stick it to the Capitol and his family, that I’m sure, as a child, he had very conflicted feelings about.

I felt his intentions were pure. The fact that he would like to stick it to the Capitol, that’s most definitely part of it. He’s most definitely entitled and privileged. But I think everyone’s given a choice, and at some point, Plutarch decided that knowledge is of the utmost importance, and I think, morally, he is very much on the side of what is right and he sees the injustice. I believe his intentions are pure. I just don’t know why you would make it all that time if your motivations were about your own self-interest.

I had a lot of conversations with some of the other actors that were playing characters that were established in the other films, and we all had our own unique challenges and thoughts about how best to capture that essence, while at the same time taking some freedom in the fact that 24-25 years is a long time. The thing that jumped out at me in the film that he was in [Hoffman first appeared in 2013’s “Catching Fire”] was that they were still most definitely fully committed to the cause, but you could see the time. You could see the weariness of all those characters.

There were little things that I was conscious of and tried to connect to his performance, but for the most part, I felt like this is a very different Plutarch. He’s beginning his journey and is incredibly ambitious, and the whole energy of his pursuit just felt very different, and all the relationships, like his relationship with Snow, are just beginning. So, it wound up feeling like there was quite a bit of room.

Most definitely. I can’t imagine where her mind will go next — no one can. But, personally, selfishly, what I would love to see is Plutarch’s teenage years. I thought a lot about that while we were shooting, but I don’t think I could play that part.

This interview has been edited and condensed for length and clarity.

All 3 The Rings Of Power Seasons, Ranked Worst To Best

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

You'd need to go back to 2003 and The Return of the King to find the last universally acclaimed Tolkien adaptation. Whether it be Peter Jackson's The Hobbit movies and The War of the Rohirrim on the big screen, or Prime Video's The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power on TV, many years have passed since Middle-earth experienced any degree of critical consensus.

The Rings of Power has proven especially divisive. The mere concept of Amazon bringing the Second Age into live-action triggered controversy by the bucket load, and opinions split further thanks to a series of significant deviations from canon: Sauron and Galadriel's romance, the 'orcs aren't so bad' angle, and Tom Bombadil: Defense Against the Dark Arts teacher.

As much as it's difficult to shower The Rings of Power with undiluted adulation, it's also impossible to deny there are elements that J.D. Payne and Patrick McKay's series does very well. This, combined with each season covering distinct periods of the Second Age's timeline, means that The Rings of Power's three seasons released so far vary considerably in quality.

The Rings of Power season 4 is confirmed and, if the showrunners have their way, a fifth and final season will follow sometime thereafter. Until then, The Rings of Power is following a very distinct trend in terms of overall quality.

The Rings of Power didn't exactly put its best foot forward at the beginning. It seems strange now, but the series initially thought the best way to adapt source material almost a century old was by following a 'mystery box' format. The Rings of Power's first season banked hard on questions like, "Who is the Stranger?" and "Who is Halbrand?" Answers to these plot points were handled like huge revelations, but in reality, the actor who looked like a young Gandalf turned out to be playing Gandalf, and the character behaving like Sauron turned out to be Sauron.

Looking back, season 1 kept its cards too close to its chest, and the whole affair ended up more akin to a Lord of the Rings imitator than a genuine Second Age TV series. The Stranger stood in for Gandalf, Adar was black metal's answer to Sauron, Nori and Poppy were your new Frodo and Sam, and Arondir covered the same bases as Legolas. Were it not for the folks in Lindon and Númenor, viewers would have been forgiven for asking whether The Rings of Power was actually a licensed Middle-earth production.

The focus on ambiguity and misdirection took the place of a compelling storyline. Not only does The Rings of Power's first season bear the least resemblance to Tolkien, it's the one season where little of note happens. The Harfoots aren't as interesting as The Rings of Power wants them to be (remember Lenny Henry was a main cast member?), Gil-galad is a shadow of himself, and Númenor is still enjoying some calm before the storm.

It's not all bad, of course. Mordor gets a genuinely fascinating origin story as the Southlands transforms into the volcanic realm The Lord of the Rings fans know and love, while Keeping Up with Khazad-dûm offers some much-needed light relief among all the wistful glances and overwrought foreshadowing.

With Sauron slipping into his Annatar guise, The Rings of Power finally found what it needed most: a canon cornerstone to build from. Wisely spending much time in Eregion, The Rings of Power's second chapter spanned the creation of the Rings of Power themselves, then ended on the Siege of Eregion, one of the Second Age's most memorable battles.

The improvement was palpable. The entire angle hinged on the chemistry between Charlie Vickers' Sauron and Charles Edwards' Celebrimbor, as the pair entered a marriage of blood, fire, and metal. The Rings of Power perfectly captured the nuances of that relationship: Celebrimbor's lofty ambition, Sauron's silky manipulations, and the authentic respect forged between them as they toiled. It also helped that, after an entire first season largely without rings, The Rings of Power finally lived up to its name in season 2, adapting a recognizable story from Tolkien's lore and being more or less faithful.

Tom Bombadil randomly popping up in Rhûn was odd. You'd almost call it fan service, but without the version of Tom fans would know from the books. Galadriel's pining over Sauron also started to grate very quickly, especially after Elrond stepped into the love triangle via a misjudged kiss. And the finale still dropped the 'Gandalf' reveal like the audience still hadn't realized Daniel Weyman wasn't just playing some random wizard.

Generally speaking, however, season 2 was where The Rings of Power started to justify its existence. There was a balance between hard-hitting battle scenes and intelligent character drama, and much less effort spent on trying to keep Tolkien readers on their toes with guessing games. Númenor was a particular highlight, as the tension between Míriel and her bearded cousin boiled over into some rather excellent political backstabbing.

One of the biggest compliments that can be paid to The Rings of Power is that it's still getting better three seasons in. After season 2 ended with the Siege of Eregion, it was left for The Rings of Power's third season to explore the War of Sauron and the Elves, while also handling the Faithful vs. King's Men civil war over in Númenor. The result was Amazon's best season of expensive Middle-earth content yet and, perhaps not coincidentally, the truest to the source material.

By some distance, Sauron shone brightest. Charlie Vickers spent the entire season attempting to construct the One Ring, and his performance exemplified the physical and emotional strain of crafting Arda's most powerful artifact this side of the Silmarils. What Tolkien described of the Ring's creation would fit onto an envelope, and The Rings of Power did admirably to stay within those lines, while also adding logical color between them. It made perfect sense that Sauron needed to know more about the Three before he could control them. And the One Ring's rhythmic method of construction went right back to the biblical opening of The Silmarillion.

The Númenor material continued the good work of season 2, depicting the Faithful as an underground rebellion, and Gil-galad finally turned into a character who vaguely resembles what Tolkien created. One can question whether renaming the Nazgûl was strictly necessary, but watching the likes of Khamûl, Theo, and the two fallen kings in the early stages of their corruption proved an absolute treat. To the east, Gandalf improved immeasurably once his arc dovetailed into the main happenings in Middle-earth.

The Rings of Power isn't quite there in terms of winning over skeptics just yet. Durin's 'wife slept with brother' storyline felt a little too soap opera-y, with very real fears of Disa shouting, "We were on a break!" at one point. The coronation of Thrain contained just a little too much Coronation Street for a Middle-earth series.

It would also be fair to argue that, for a season revolving entirely around two massive wars, The Rings of Power season 3 contained very little war. Opening battle aside, you'd never know conflict was even afoot. Indeed, The Rings of Power twice pulled back from battle scenes at the last minute: once when news of Míriel's wedding prevented the Faithful charging against the King's Men, and then again when Sauron's surrender avoided a clash between his forces and the Númenor-Elf alliance. Hopefully the battle budget is being saved for season 5.

Nevertheless, The Rings of Power sails toward its fourth season with the critical winds at its back, and the gradual improvement seen since the beginning offers reason for optimism that Sauron's trip to Númenor will not disappoint.

‘Stranger Things’ Star's ‘Hellboy’ Reboot Officially Leaves Netflix in 5 Days

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Mike Mignola's monster-hunting demon Hellboy has graced the big screen four times now. The most controversial take on the character can currently be found on Netflix, but you'd better act fast, or it'll be dragged to Hell. David Harbour donned the crimson prosthetics to portray the character, presaging his current success in the Marvel Cinematic Universe.

Mignola created Hellboy in 1994 as a creator-owned character; he has gone on to become one of the greatest horror comic successes of the modern era, even after Mignola concluded his story in Hellboy in Hell. The character first graced the screen in 2004 in Guillermo del Toro's Hellboy: Ron Perlman played the title character with his customary aplomb, while Doug Jones played his aquatic ally Abe Sapien, presaging his amphibious star turn in The Shape of Water, and Selma Blair starred as Hellboy's pyrokinetic ex Liz Sherman. The trio reprised their roles in 2008's Hellboy II: The Golden Army; a third film was mooted, but del Toro moved on and Perlman was unwilling to make the film without him, and so the franchise was rebooted.

2019's Hellboy stars Stranger Things' Harbour as the title character, a demon who's been raised in the bosom of the Bureau for Paranormal Research and Defense by Professor Trevor Bruttenholm (Ian McShane, Deadwood). He teams with BPRD were-jaguar Ben Daimio (Daniel Dae Kim, Lost) and medium Alice Monaghan (Sasha Lane, Twisters) in a race against time as the porcine fairy Gruagach (Stephen Graham, Adolescence) attempts to reassemble the dismembered body of the sorceress Vivienne Nimue (Milla Jovovich, Resident Evil). Can they stop her resurrection before she brings about the End Times? You've got until November 30, when Hellboy leaves Netflix, to find out.

Four worlds and four completely different ideas of what a good life looks like. This is where you belong, what it would ask of you, and what you would get back in return.

You belong somewhere old enough that the mountains have names and somebody can still tell you who gave them. Middle-earth is for people who think the right thing is worth doing even when it will not be finished in their lifetime.

You want the sea, a crew, and absolutely nobody telling you where you are allowed to go. The Grand Line is the world for people whose main ambition in life is finding out what is over there.

You would rather be feared and useful than liked and useless. The Continent is a world where the monsters are the straightforward part of the job and the people are where it all gets complicated.

You want the myths to be real and loose in the actual world, and you want to find out at about thirteen that you were never anywhere near as ordinary as everybody kept telling you.

Hellboy was not the success the studio was hoping for with critics, fans, or audiences. The next iteration of the franchise took shape five years later, with Hellboy: The Crooked Man. An adaptation of a miniseries from Mignola and late horror comics legend Richard Corben, The Crooked Man took a stripped-down approach to the material, with a low budget and a largely unknown cast: Jack Kesy (Deadpool 2) stars as Hellboy, who finds himself in the backwoods of Appalachia as he hunts a monstrous creature. It was released in theaters internationally and direct-to-VOD in the US in 2024.

Hellboy is leaving Netflix on November 30. Stay tuned to Collider for future updates.

Prediction: These 3 Consumer Stocks Will Beat the S&P 500 Over the Next 10 Years

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

I think Ferrari, Hilton, and Amer Sports have the brands, the growth runways, and the discipline to outrun the S&P 500 over the next decade.

By Micah Zimmerman – Nov 26, 2026 at 5:30PM EST

Beating the S&P 500 sounds simple until you remember how good the index already is, since it has compounded at about 9.9% a year on a total return basis going back to 1928. So a stock that beats the index over 10 years needs more than a good quarter, it needs a business that can keep growing over the long term.

When I look for that kind of staying power in the consumer world, I aim for three things: a brand that people pay up for, a long expansion runway, and a management team that protects both. Here are three consumer stocks that I think long-term investors should consider for the decade ahead.

Ferrari (RACE +2.96%) deliberately builds fewer cars than people want, and that scarcity is the heart of its economic moat, which is a durable advantage that keeps rivals from stealing its customers.

CEO Benedetto Vigna said in the third-quarter release that deliveries "reflect our clients' ever-deepening desire to make each Ferrari uniquely their own." Those personal touches add profit to every car without adding volume.

Demand isn't the problem. Vigna said on the call, "We continue to experience a healthy demand across all geographies, with an order book that enters 2028."

The risks are a premium valuation, at roughly 36 times earnings, and the shift to electric models like the Luce, which has to win over buyers who love engines.

Hilton (HLT +0.52%) mostly doesn't own its hotels; it collects fees by managing and licensing its brands to owners through a franchise model, which is an arrangement where a business pays to use another company's brand and systems. That lets it grow without spending heavily on buildings.

CEO Chris Nassetta said in the third-quarter release, "We achieved our highest quarter ever for organic openings." He also said on the call, "Globally, Hilton is still less than 6% of supply providing a long runway for continued growth."

Travel is cyclical, though. CFO Kevin Jacobs said, "RevPAR in China declined 5.4% in the quarter," referring to revenue per available room, which is a key hotel measure of how well rooms are filled and priced. Middle East conflict is weighing on that region, too, and the stock trades near 49 times earnings.

Amer Sports (AS +2.56%) owns Arc'teryx jackets, Salomon shoes, and Wilson tennis gear, and every segment grew by double digits in its latest quarter. CEO James Zheng said, "I am very confident in the future outlook for our company"

The U.S. opportunity is what excites me most. Arc'teryx CEO Stuart Haselden noted that the brand has about as many stores in the U.S. as in Canada, which he said "makes it very apparent the potential that we have to expand in the U.S." Management also said Europe's store traffic is a challenge, and higher freight costs are nibbling at margins.

None of these companies will beat the market every year, but I think long-term investors should consider owning all three and giving their growth stories the full decade they deserve. Great brands tend to compound quietly.

What ties these three together is pricing power, which is the ability to raise prices without losing customers, and I think that matters more over a decade than any single quarter's results.

Ferrari's buyers wait years for their cars, Hilton's owners keep signing up for its brands, and Arc'teryx shoppers happily pay premium prices for gear that lasts.

There will be rough stretches, whether it's a travel slowdown, a weak luxury market, or a cold winter for outdoor sales, but investors who hold through those dips give each business time to prove its case. In the end, patience is the edge most investors give up too easily.

Pedro Porro puts it back on Barça and Real

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Since the end of last season, Pedro Porro (27 years old) is often cited to come and strengthen FC Barcelona or Real Madrid. Holder with Spain and author of a major World Cup, the right back attracts the attention of the big leagues. It will still be necessary to reach an agreement with Tottenham, where it extended only a few weeks ago, until 2031. The English club will demand at least €70 million to let it go. “I have a contract with my club and I am very happy there. I have to respect that. Of course, it’s football: there are always unforeseen events, rumors circulating, but for now, I’m focusing on the national team and then we’ll see», he responds to Sport during this international break.

He is of course not insensitive to the rumors circulating, particularly those leading him to the Blaugrana. “It’s flattering. When one of the best clubs in the world is interested in you, it’s always a good sign, because it probably means I’m doing my job well. I must continue on this path», he continues. Porro was even praised by Joan Laporta himself during an interview a few weeks ago, saying he was “one of his favorite players.» «I hadn’t seen it. It’s also a compliment from him. As I said previously, it’s a good sign when, for example, the president of a club other than mine appreciates my work. This definitely means I’m on the right track.» To the wise.

Saatva vs Tempur-Pedic vs DreamCloud: Which mattress topper should side sleepers buy in Black Friday sales? Tempur-Pedic is the best we've tested, but the DreamCloud is ideal for hip pain

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Each of these mattress toppers softened up our hard beds, but there's a clear winner for the ultimate in cooling side sleeper comfort (and it has a big Black Friday price cut too)

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

While Saatva, Tempur-Pedic, and DreamCloud are offering the best Black Friday mattress deals we've seen this year, all three brands are also behind some of 2026's best mattress toppers. But which premium mattress topper should side sleepers buy in the Black Friday sales?

We've tested the Saatva Graphite, Tempur-Adapt + Cooling and the DreamCloud mattress toppers and they each deliver the signature luxury of their respective brands' mattresses. This comparison guide will help you choose which topper is right for you if you're a side sleeper in need of extra comfort.

For the latest price cuts, also see our guide to the best Black Friday mattress deals live.

A comfy topper from a leading sleep brand, but its retention of excess heat lets it down. Though if feel cold in bed, this could be ideal.

The 3"-thick Graphite Memory Foam Mattress Topper is the most popular entry in Saatva's bed topper collection. Its body-cradling foam supports all sleep positions, including side sleeping, while its anchor straps keep the topper in place. However, despite the infusion of graphite, this memory foam topper can trap body heat.

Sink-in softness, contouring that hugs your curves to relieve pressure, and all-night cooling to keep you at the right temperature for sleep.

The Tempur-Adapt + Cooling is the most top-rated mattress topper we've ever tested, as it uses Tempur-Pedic's propriety Tempur-Material to provide softening comfort for side sleepers and hard beds. The addition of a cooling cover prevents heat build-up, but be aware that there's no official sleep trial.

A good cooling memory foam topper to soften the area under your shoulders, hips and knees, but the lack of anchor straps is frustrating.

The DreamCloud Softer Mattress Topper is made from a 2" slab of gel memory foam that our testers found to be cushioning along the shoulders and hips, making it ideal for side sleepers. Its breathable cover complete with "cooling yarns" means the DreamCloud Topper doesn't overheat. However, there's no anchor straps to prevent shifting.

All three mattress toppers are 3" thick and use memory foam to cushion firm beds. While each works well for side sleepers, their distinct features make them better suited for different types of side sleepers.

For instance, the Saatva Graphite Memory Foam Mattress Topper caters to all sleep styles, making it ideal for side sleepers who sometimes switch to back or front sleeping — or side sleepers who share a bed with someone who sleeps in a different position. In contrast, the DreamCloud and Tempur-Adapt have a plush feel best suited to side sleepers only.

The Tempur-Adapt + Cooling Mattress Topper has a cooling cover to prevent side sleepers from overheating (the Saatva Graphite, on the other hand, is prone to heat build-up). The DreamCloud Softer Mattress Topper also sleeps cooler than the Saatva Graphite, but doesn't have the washable cover with anchor straps. Still, it's the cheapest of the three, starting at $179 (the Saatva starts at $225; the Tempur-Adapt at $259).

We test mattress toppers in key areas, particularly in temperature regulation and comfort and support. We then give each area a score out of 5.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

When testing comfort and support, our testers sleep on each topper in the three main sleep styles (back, side, and stomach) to see how comfortable and supportive it is in each position. The Saatva Graphite performed the best in this area, scoring a near-perfect 4.5/5, because its medium-firm feel supports all sleep styles.

In comparison, the Tempur-Adapt's 3.5/5 score may seem low, but that's because it's too soft to support back and front sleepers. For side sleepers only, the Tempur-Adapt + Cooling provides incredible pressure relief. The DreamCloud also lost a point for its softness, but it's still a great topper for side sleepers.

As for temperature regulation (how well a topper maintains a comfortable temperature), the Tempur-Adapt's cooling cover upgrade scored the highest points. Meanwhile, the DreamCloud's breathable cover, while not cool-to-the-touch, prevented overheating. The Saatva Graphite scored the lowest, as our lead tester found it was prone to trapping heat.

Here's a close-up look at each mattress topper, exploring their respective designs and prices, plus who we do and don't recommend them to...

The Saatva Graphite Memory Foam Mattress Topper is a 3" slab of graphite-infused memory foam that's enveloped in a breathable, antimicrobial organic cotton cover (the same material used for the covers of the best Saatva mattresses).

Despite a naturally cool cover and a graphite-infusion, our testers found the Saatva Graphite's memory foam to trap heat. However, both Saatva customers and our testers agree that the memory foam's medium-firm feel adds plenty of comfort and support to mattresses.

The Saatva Graphite (or any other Saatva toppers, for that matter) are pretty much never included in the Saatva Black Friday sales, let alone the monthly Saatva sales. That means you'll always pick up a Saatva Graphite from $225 in a twin. Extras include a generous 180-night sleep trial and a so-so one-year warranty.

The 3" Temur-Adapt + Cooling Mattress Topper is actually an upgrade of the original Tempur-Adapt Mattress Topper. We found the original's use of pressure-relieving Tempur-Material was great for side sleeping, but was also prone to heat build-up.

That's why were so pleased when the + Cooling upgrade added a cooling cover, with out lead tester impressed with the difference it made. While not a great mattress topper for those who sleep on their back or front, it's a top pick for side sleepers who need to soften hard beds.

The Tempur-Adapt + Cooling Topper is the only one out of the three toppers to be included in the Black Friday mattress topper sales. This means you'll save up to 30% on all sizes in this month's Tempur-Pedic sales, with a twin now down to $259 (was $339). Disappointingly, there's no sleep trial, but you will get a generous 10-year warranty.

DreamCloud is known for its flagship DreamCloud Classic Hybrid, a luxury hybrid mattress at a medium-range price, so it's only fitting that it would offer a bed topper at a fraction of the cost of its mattresses, too.

The DreamCloud Softer Topper is one of two DreamCloud toppers; the other is called the DreamCloud Firmer Topper, which is designed to give a (you guessed it) a firmer feel. The DreamCloud Softer Topper is made from 2" of gel-infused memory foam, which our testers say provides a contouring feel for side sleepers.

The DreamCloud Softer Topper may not be on sale for Black Friday (no surprises there, it's never in the DreamCloud sales) but that doesn't stop it from being the cheapest option. A twin is priced at $179, and comes with a 100-night sleep trial, a one-year warranty, and free shipping and returns.

For side sleepers who are looking to soften a hard bed, we'd reccomend looking at the Temour-Adapt + Cooling or the DreamCloud Softer Topper. Both have a plusher feel than the Graphite and are great at regulating temperature, though those who need a bigger comfort boost should go for the thicker Tempur-Adapt.

Those looking for the most cost-effective, on the other hand, should pick the under-$200-for-a-queen DreamCloud. Meanwhile, the Saatva Graphite is best for side sleepers who prefer a firmer feel, combination sleepers, and couples with different sleep styles.

Here is where the Lions fit in the NFC playoff picture after their Thanksgiving loss

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

The Detroit Lions' devastating and frustrating loss to the Chicago Bears drops them out of first place in the NFC North. It gives the Bears sole possession of first place in the division at 9-2, no matter what happens in the rest of Week 12. Detroit will hold at second place, even if the Minnesota Vikings defeat the Atlanta Falcons on Sunday.

Despite the disappointing outcome, the Lions are 8-3 and still control their own destiny in the division. With a win in over the Bears in Week 17, they can even the teams' record and tie the head-to-head tiebreaker. Detroit is currently ahead in the NFC North division record tiebreaker (2-1 vs. 1-2), so if Detroit runs the table or matches Chicago, the Lions get the tiebreaker.

Here's a full look at the NFC playoff picture following the Thanksgiving Eve game and the first Thanksgiving Day game:

Pride of Detroit is SB Nation's home for Lions news, analysis, and conversation. Download the app to connect with fellow Detroit diehards and share your take.

5. Detroit Lions (8-3)6. Minnesota Vikings (7-3)7. Los Angeles Rams (7-4)

8. Seattle Seahawks (6-4)9. Green Bay Packers (6-5)10. Dallas Cowboys (5-6)11. Washington Commanders (3-5-1)12. Tampa Bay Buccaneers (3-7)13. Atlanta Falcons (3-7)14. New Orleans Saints (3-7)15. Philadelphia Eagles (3-7)16. Arizona Cardinals (3-8)

The Packers' loss on Thanksgiving Eve drops them outside the current playoff picture, but things are getting tight in a hurry. While the Lions still hold their own destiny, a Seahawks win would mean they are just a single game from being outside the playoff picture entirely.

Detroit has a chance to rebound in the upcoming weeks with games against the Falcons and Titans, but with three NFC North games remaining—all on the road—a lot can happen.
